COMP202 Week 1 - solutions to exercises
Question 1. John gave me the following argument, claiming to prove that
n2 = O(n). What is the flaw in John’s argument?
Attempted proof by John
I’ll show this by induction on n. Essentially, I’ll show that n2 is always
at most a constant times n, which is what n2 = O(n) means.
• The inductive hypothesis: n2 is at most a constant times n.
• The base case is clearly fine since the inductive hypothesis is true
for n = 1.
• Inductive step: Assume the inductive hypothesis is true for n =
k ≥ 1. That is, k2 ≤ ck for a constant c. I’m now going to show
that the inductive hypothesis is also true for k + 1. That is, I’ll
show that (k + 1)2 is also at most a constant times k + 1, which
will prove what we want.
(k +1)2 = k2 +2k +1 expanding the square
≤ ck +2k +1 as we assumed hypothesis true for n = k
≤ ck +3k since 1 ≤ k
= (c+3)k collecting common terms
≤ (c+3)(k +1). as k ≤ k +1
But since c was a constant, so is c+3, so (k+1)2 is at most (c+3)
times k +1.
Thus, n2 = O(n).
Solution: Recall from the definition that f(n) = O(g(n)) means there are
constants c and n such that:
0
f(n) ≤ c·g(n) for all n ≥ n .
0
1
--- Page 2 ---
Crucially, note that c must be independent of n (i.e., one cannot vary the
value of the constant depending on n), which is not the case in the
attempted proof where k2 ≤ ck but (k+1)2 ≤ (c+3)(k+1): the value of ‘c’
for k +1 is larger than that for k. In other words, the induction hypothesis
has not been presented formally enough: one must change it to something of
the form “n2 ≤ cn for all n ≥ 2”, but this is not true as observed above. □

n→∞ g(n)
is said to be ω(g(n)) if lim f(n) = ∞ (or equivalently, if g(n) is o(f(n))).
n→∞ g(n)
Prove that n! = ω(2n) and n! = o(nn).
Solution:
•
n! n·(n−1)···1 n n−1 1 n
= = · ··· ≥ ,
2n 2·2···2 2 2 2 4
which goes to infinity as n → ∞.
•
n! n·(n−1)···1 n n−1 1 1
= = · ··· ≤ ,
nn n·n···n n n n n
which goes to 0 as n → ∞.
□

n for all n ≥ 1, and hence, loglogn ≤ logn for all n ≥ 2.
√
Also, we know that logn ≤ n for all n ≥ 1. If you know any calculus,
this is easy to prove. First show that lim logn = 0 using de L’Hˆopital’s
n→∞ n
Rule. This means that logn = o(n), and, as mentioned in class, this implies
that logn = O(n). (I basically said that you can assume the fact that
logn = O(n), and that logn = O(n1) for any fixed k ≥ 1.)
k

large enough n. Similarly, n3logn ≤ n4 ≤ n5 − n4 for “large enough” n.
These statements can be more formally proved.

Question 4. Prove by mathematical induction that Algorithm Minimum-
Element from the introductory lectures is correct.
Solution: We argue by induction that for any integer j ≥ 1, the algorithm
has in variable min the minimum in the sub-array A[1..j]. Induction base
for j = 1 is obviously true due to line 1.
For the induction step, suppose for the induction hypothesis, that min
has the current minimum in the subarray A[1..j] and consider iteration j+1
in the for loop. If in line 6 we have that A[j + 1] < min, then that means
that the minimum of the sub-array A[1..j +1] is in fact A[j +1], which the
algorithm correctly updates in line 7. Otherwise, the current value of min is
also minimum in the sub-array A[1..j+1]. Thus after j+1-st iteration, min
has the corrent minimum in sub-array A[1..j+1]. This finishes the inductive
argument.
Now, using this fact for j = length[A], we conclude that the algorithm
returns as final min the correct minimum in the whole array. □
3

1. Givealinear(O(n))runningtimealgorithmforthemaximum-subarray
problem. Hint: Try a non-recursive algorithm.
Solution: Let A be the input array. Scan A from left to right. In the
j’th step, store the largest subarray sum that ends (and includes!) the
j’th element.
1: Input: A = [a 1 ,...,a n ].
2: bestsum ← −∞
3: currentsum = 0.
4: for i = 1 to n do
5: currentsum = max(a i ,currentsum+a i )
6: bestsum = max(bestsum,currentsum)
7: end for
8: return bestsum
Loop invariant: After the i’th step, currentsum holds the solution to
the maximum-subarray problem for input [a ,...,a ] with the addi-
1 i
tionalrestrictionthata mustbeincluded. Thevariablebestsumholds
i
the solution to the maximum-subarray problem for input [a ,...,a ].
1 i
Proof: Thiscanbeprovedbyinduction. Thebasecaseiseasy. Inthe
j’th step, the old value of currentsum holds max {a +···+
i∈{1,...,j−1} i
a }. That is, before the updates on Line 5 and Line 6, currentsum
j−1
holdsthesolutiontothemaximum-subarrayproblemfor[a ,...,a ]
1 j−1
where a must be included. Thus, after the updates, currentsum
j−1
equals max {a +···+a ,a }, which equals the solution to
i∈{1,...,j−1} i j j
the maximum-subarray problem for input [a ,...,a ] with the restric-
1 j
tion that a must be included. Moreover, it can be seen that bestsum
j
stores the value of the best solution seen so far, which is the solution
to the maximum-subarray problem for input [a ,...,a ].

2. Describe a divide-and-conquer algorithm to find the minimum of a
set of n numbers. Your algorithm should clearly describe how the
problem is divided into subproblems. Either give some psuedo-code
or describe your algorithm clearly in words. Assume that the set is
stored in an array A = [A[1],...,A[n]].
1
--- Page 2 ---
(Onlyadivide-and-conqueralgorithmwillbeacceptedhere. Ofcourse
youcanfindtheminimumintimeO(n),butthepointistounderstand
the divide-and-conquer paradigm.)
What is the running time of your algorithm?
Solution: I hope that one idea for a solution is pretty clear here.
What do you do?
Divide the array A into two (nearly equal) parts. Find the minimum
of each part (recursively). So you’ll have min and min which are the
ℓ r
minimums of the “left” and “right” sublists. Then the minimum of
the whole list is the smaller of min and min .
ℓ r
Suppose, for the sake of convenience that n is a power of 2, i.e. n = 2k
for some non-negative integer k.
At level i (in the recursion) you will have a collection of 2i sets, each
of size n/2i. So on the “bottom” level, you’ll end up with n/2 pairs
of numbers. You’ll find the minimum of each pair (using a single
comparison) to give you a set of n/2 numbers as you work your way
back up the recursive calls.
Then you’ll perform n/4 comparisons to end up with n/4 numbers,
and so forth. As you work your way back up the recursive calls, at
level i you will have reduced the list down to a set of n/2k−i numbers,
which you have divided into pairs and you will find the minimum of
each pair.
In total, how many comparisons will you perform? As I said, if you
usethisidea, you’llperformn/2comparisons, thenn/4, thenn/8, ...,
then finally a single comparison to finish finding the minimum. So the
total number of comparisons is
n/2+n/4+···+n/n = 1+2+4+8+···+n/2
= 1+21+22+23+···+2k−1
= 2k −1
(using the fact that
(cid:80)k−1ci
=
ck−1.)
i=0 c−1
So the total running tims of this algorithm is O(2k) = O(n). The time
consuming part of the task will be in constructing the sublists. At
each level, constructing the smaller lists will take constant time O(1),
because it only need to pass to the recursive calls of the algorithm the
start and end index of each smaller subarray. Similar calculation as
for the number of comparisons, shows that the total running time is
also O(n).
2
--- Page 3 ---
However, observe that we obtain a very precise bound on the total
numberofcomparisons. Namely,thealgorithmusesatmostis2k−1 =
n−1 comparisons! That is, we do not use here the big-Oh notation,
because the constant factor in front of n−1 is one.
3. Use the Master Method to find an asymptotic expression for T(n).
(Assume that T(n) = c for n ≤ d, for some constants c > 0 and
d ≥ 1.)
Solution: Note that in all cases here, you want to consider the func-
tionnlog b a andcomparethis tothefunctionf(n)inasuitable fashion.
(a) T(n) = 2T(n/2)+logn
Here we have that nlog b a = nlog 2 2 = n. Recalling that I stated
in class that logn is O(n1/k) for all k ≥ 1, we note that logn is
O(n1/2). In other words, there is an ε > 0, for instance ε = 1/2,
such that f(n) = logn is O(n(log b a)−ε) = O(n1−ε) = O(n1/2).
So we’re in Case 1, and T(n) is Θ(n).
(b) T(n) = 7T(n/3)+n2
First note that log a = log 7 < 2 (strictly smaller than 2).
b 3
Therefore, we can find ε > 0 (very small but positive), such that
f(n) = n2 is Ω(nlog 3 7+ε). Also, we note that 7·(n/3)2 = (7/9)n2,
with δ = 7/9 < 1 (strictly smaller than 1), we see that we’re in
Case 3.
Therefore T(n) is Θ(n2).
(c) T(n) = 9T(n/3)+n3logn
Since log b a = log 3 9 = 2, we’re comparing nlog b a = n2 and
f(n) = n3logn. Obviously, we can again find ε > 0 (like ε = 1,
say) so that n3logn is Ω(n2+ε). So we seem to be in Case 3.
Look now at a·f(n/b). This is af(n/b) = 9·(n/3)3log(n/3) =
(1/3)n3log(n/3) ≤ (1/3)n3logn = δ·f(n). (Why is this last in-
equalitytrue?) Wecanthereforeuseδ = 1/3 < 1inourcondition
for Case 3.
So T(n) is Θ(n3logn).
(d) T(n) = 16T(n/2)+(nlogn)4
nlog b a = nlog 4 16 = n4. Since f(n) = (nlogn)4 = n4log4n, we are
in Case 2 this time with k = 4 in the Master Method Theorme.
(That is, n4log4n is obviously Θ(n4log4n).)
So we conclude that T(n) is Θ(n4log5n). (Note the increase in
the exponent from k = 4 to k + 1 = 5 on the logarithm that
comes from the Master Theorem.)
3
--- Page 4 ---
(e) T(n) = 4T(n/2)+n2
Here we’re comparing nlog b a = nlog 2 4 = n2 and f(n) = n2. Like
the previous example right before this one, we’re in Case 2 with
k = 0 this time, because f(n) = Θ(nlog b alogkn) = Θ(nlog b a) =
Θ(n2) (note that logkn = log0n = 1).
This means that T(n) is Θ(n2logk+1n) = Θ(n2logn).
(f) T(n) = 2T(n/2)+10n
Since log b a = log 2 2 = 1, so we’re comparing nlog b a = n1 = n
and f(n) = 10n. So we’re again in Case 2 with k = 0 in this
instance too.
Therefore T(n) is Θ(nlogn).
(g) T(n) = 8T(n/2)+1000n2
log b a = log 2 8 = 3, so we compare nlog b a = n3 and f(n) =
1000n2. We’re in Case 1 since we can find 0 < ε ≤ 1 such that
1000n2 is O(n3−ε) (we can use here for instance ε = 0.9 or even
ε = 1).
This means that T(n) is Θ(n3).
√
(h) T(n) = 3T(n/3)+ n
Again we’re in Case 1 here since we’re comparing nlog 3 3 = n and
√ √
f(n) = n. Now take any 0 < ε ≤ 1, and we have that n is
2
O(nlog 3 3−ε = n1−ε).
Therefore, T(n) is Θ(n).
(i) T(n) = 2T(n/2)+nlogn
This satisfies Case 2 with k = 1 (why?). So T(n) is Θ(nlog2n).
(j) T(n) = 3T(n/2)+n2
This recurrence satisfies Case 3 with, say, δ = 3. (Why?)
4
So T(n) is Θ(n2).
(k) T(n) = T(n/2)+2n
This is also an example of Case 3. Certainly there is ε > 0 such
that f(n) = 2n is Ω(n(log 2 1)+ε) = Ω(nε) (note that log 2 1 = 0).
(Whatisavalueofδ thatwouldworkherefortheothercondition
on f(n) = 2n?)
In this case we have that T(n) is Θ(2n).

COMP202 Week 3 - solutions
1. A biased coin comes up heads with probability p, where 0 < p < 1.
The coin is flipped repeatedly until the first head appears.
Let X denote the number of flips made (including the final flip that
gives heads).
Compute E[X].
Solution: This solution is similar to the analysis we did in class for
the case p = 1/2. Consider the first coin flip.
• With probability p, the flip is heads and the experiment ends
after one flip.
• With probability 1−p, the flip is tails; this costs one flip, and we
are now in exactly the same situation as at the start.
Therefore,
E[X] = p·1+(1−p)·(1+E[X]).
Simplifying,
E[X] = 1+(1−p)E[X],
so
pE[X] = 1,
and hence
1
E[X] = .
p
Intuition: If heads is unlikely (that is, p is small), we expect to wait
a long time before seeing one. If p = 1/2, we recover the familiar value
E[X] = 2. If p is close to 1, the expected number of flips is close to 1,
which matches our intuition.
2. Two fair six-sided dice are rolled once. Let D = |X − Y| be the
absolute difference between the two outcomes.
Compute E[D].

value would be larger than 6, which is impossible) and set the larger
value to s+d. This gives 6−d outcomes with X < Y and the same

3. Bob has a set A of n nuts and a set B of n bolts, such that each nut
in A has a unique matching bolt in B. Unfortunately, the nuts in A
all look the same, and the bolts in B all look the same as well. The
only kind of comparison that Bob can make is to take a nut-bolt pair
(a,b) (such that a ∈ A and b ∈ B) and test them to see if the threads
of a are larger, smaller, or a perfect match with the threads of b.
Describe an efficient algorithm for Bob to match up all of the nuts
and bolts. What is the running time of this algorithm, in terms of the
number of nut-bolt comparisons that Bob must make?
2
--- Page 3 ---
Solution: You should be used to this by now. Apply the randomized
QuickSort algorithm in the following manner.
Pick a nut (at random), use this to sort the bolts into two sets, those
havingdiameterssmallerthanthenutandlargerthanthenut, finding
the matching bolt in the process. (You can repeat this process if you
like, until you have the two sets being “not too big” as in the normal
randomized QuickSort routine.)
Now that you have found one matching nut/bolt pair, use the bolt
to sort the nuts into two piles, those with threads larger or smaller
than the bolt. Now you have two pile of nuts and two piles of bolts.
note that since each bolt has a matching nut, and vice-versa (and the
matches are unique), then the pile of bolts with the “smaller” threads
is equal in size to the pile of nuts with the “smaller” threads, and the
same is true of the other pile of nuts and bolts.
Then you repeat this process on each of the matching piles of nuts and
bolts.
UsingaversionoftherandomizedQuickSortproceduretodothis,how
many comparisons will you have to make?
If we use the randomized procedure to choose our “pivots” to ensure
the piles aren’t too big, then, in expectation since it’s a randomized
procedure, we’ll be doing at most O(nlogn) comparisons. The first
round takes n+n comparisons (well, it’s really n+(n−1)), so there’s
O(n) comparisons there.
The second round we’ll do at most O(n) comparisons again. (Recall,
the randomization procedure ensures that each subset is size at most
3n. So we would do at most 23n comparisons to subdivide the match-
4 4
ing set of nuts and bolts into two smaller piles. thus overall we’ll do
at most n comparisons again).
Thenumberofroundsuntilwe’redonewillbe(inexpectation)atmost
log n, as in the case of the usual randomized QuickSort procedure.
4
3
So overall we’ll have at most (in expectation) O(nlogn) comparisons.
4. You are given an unsorted array A of n integers and an integer T.
Describeanefficientalgorithmtodeterminewhetherthemedian (that
is, the n/2’th largest element) of A is strictly greater than T.
Solution: Asshouldhopefullybeclear,youcandirectlyuseRandom-
ized Select to get the n/2’th largest number in expected O(n) queries,
and just check if that number is larger than T.
However, there’s a much simpler solution to this problem that just
requires a linear scan: maintain a counter that counts the number of
3
--- Page 4 ---
items seen that are larger than T. Initialize the counter to 0, and
simply scan the list from left to right, increasing the counter by 1
whenever you see an element larger than T. If this counter exceeds
n/2 answer yes, else answer no.
The key difference that makes this problem so easy in contrast with
Selection is that we’re given the number T in this problem.
4

COMP202 Week 4 - solutions
Note: As you might suspect, most of these exercises can be solved
essentially using variations of efficient sorting algorithms (e.g., MergeSort
or HeapSort or (randomized) QuickSort).
1. SupposethatS isasetofnintegers. Describeanefficientalgorithmto
determine whether there are two (or more) equal integers in S. What
is the running time of your algorithm?
(Note: This can easily be done in time O(n2). (How?) Can you do
better?)
Solution: We start performing the MergeSort or HeapSort or ran-
domized Quicksort algorithm.
Supposewe’reusingtheMergeSortalgorithm. Then, aswe’remerging
thelists(orsomeofthesublistsinoneoftherecursivecalls),wemerely
check to see if the two items we’re comparing as we merge the lists
are equal to each other. If so, we can stop and report “Yes”. If we
never find an equal pair of integers, then when we’re finished with the
sorting, we report “No”.
For QuickSort, observe that it does not do “merging” of sublists, but
just”appends”the(recursivelysorted)L(elementstrictlysmallerthan
pivot)onlefttoE(elementsequaltopivot)inthemiddleandappends
that to (recursively sorted) R (element strictly larger than pivot) on
the right.
The solution with the randomized QuickSort algorithm is similar to
MergeSort, but when we’re creating the sublists using the pivot ele-
ment, if the sublist we called E is ever non-empty at some step, then
we again report “Yes”. More precisely: if in any level of recursion the
set E has more than element, then we output “Yes” (I assume that at
the bottom of recursion arrays have length 1), and we output “No” if
every set E has only one element.
Using the first method takes time O(nlogn) in the worst case as
we are basically performing the MergeSort algorithm. The second
method takes expected time O(nlogn) using the randomized Quick-
Sort method.
1
--- Page 2 ---
If you want to use HeapSort, this is also fine. Simply maintain a vari-
able that stores the last number removed by the RemoveMax opera-
tion. Notethataduplicateinasetmeansinsortedordertheremustbe
2 same values occuring next to each other. All you need to check now
is whether during the execution of HeapSort there’s any RemoveMax
operation that returns the same value of the previous RemoveMax
operation. This only adds an overhead of O(1) per RemoveMax op-
eration, so the total worst-case running time of this procedure is also
O(nlogn).
2. Suppose you are given two sequences X and Y of k integers each,
possibly containing duplicates. Describe an efficient algorithm to de-
termine if X and Y contain the same set of elements (possibly in
different orders). What is the running time of your procedure?
(Note: There are two ways this could be interpreted. Suppose that
X = (1,1,1,1,2) and Y = (2,2,1,2,1). If we take only the collection
of distinct elements into account, then since X and Y both contain
1 and 2 (only “counting” a duplicated item once), we might consider
them to “contain the same set of elements”. If, on the other hand, we
want to consider both the elements and the number of occurrences,
then X and Y are do not contain the same set of elements, as the
number of occurrences of 1 and 2 in the lists are clearly different.
Consider both of these cases in your analysis.)
Solution: Surprise, surprise... Apply the MergeSort algorithm on
each list X and Y separately. Then when we’re done withsorting each
list, we compare them element by element to see if they are equal.
The only difference between the two interpretations is how we might
consider merging the sublists. If we want to exclude the duplicates to
consider X and Y equal, then we need only include one copy when
we merge the lists. In other words, if we have X = (1,1,1,1,2) and
Y = (2,2,1,2,1), when we sort and “merge” the lists, we can end up
with the new lists X′ = (1,2) and Y′ = (1,2) which we compare and
see they are equal.
If we wish to include the duplicates, then when we sort the lists we
will end up with (1,1,1,1,2) and (1,1,2,2,2) which we then compare
element by element and see they are different lists.
In any case, depending upon how we wish to compare the lists, we can
still do this in time O(nlogn), as we apply MergeSort twice (or some
small variation of it if we remove the duplicates).
2
--- Page 3 ---
3. Let A and B be two sequences, each having k integers. Given an

Solution I: We will first show a non-recursive solution. First uning
any sorting algorithm (e.g., MergeSort, HeapSort), sort one of these

total time is O(klogk). Then take sequentially each element a from
tableAandcheckbybinarysearchifnumberx−aispresentinB; this
takes time O(logk). If the answer is yes then we can stop, otherwise
we continue. The total time of this part is the number of elements in
A times O(logk), that is, O(klogk).

4. You are given an array A of n integers, where each element lies in the
range {1,2,...,k}.
DescribeanalgorithmthatrunsintimeO(n+k)todeterminewhether
any value appears more than n/3 times in A.
Solution: We don’t use Counting Sort directly, but we use a subrou-
tine that it employs. Here is the algorithm.
• Create an array C[1..k] and initialise all entries to zero.
• Scan the array A. During the scan, for each element a ∈ A,
increment C[a].
• ScanthearrayC. Ifanyentryisstrictlygreaterthann/3,output
“Yes”; otherwise output “No”.
A sketch of correctness: For each value i ∈ {1,...,k}, the array C
stores exactly the number of times i appears in A. The algorithm
therefore outputs “Yes” if and only if there exists a value that appears
more than n/3 times in A.
The first scan of A takes O(n) time. The scan of C takes O(k) time.
Hence the total running time is O(n+k).

3. (a) Describeanefficientgreedyalgorithmformakingchangeforaspeci-
fiedvalueusingaminimum numberofcoins,assumingthatthereare
four denominations of currency of coins with values 27,9,3,1.
Argue why your algorithm is correct.
(b) Consider a post office that sells stamps in three different denomi-
nations, 1p, 7p, and 10p. Show that the natural greedy algorithm
(hopefully the one you designed as a solution to the previous ques-
tion) won’t necessarily give a right answer by exhibiting an input
whereitgoeswrong. Designadynamicprogrammingalgorithmthat
will find the minimum number of stamps necessary for a postage
value of N pence.
What is the running time of your algorithm?
Solution:
(a) The algorithm to use here is the obvious one: Given a target value
N, take as many 27s as possible until you can take no more, then
take as many 9s, etc. So the algorithm is “greedy” in the sense that
you take as large a coin as possible until you can’t take any more,
2
--- Page 3 ---
then you move onto the next largest coin. This will eventually give
you a number of coins that will add up to the given value N.
Why is this correct, in the sense that it will give you the minimum
number of coins whose value sums to the given value in question?
An obvious first observation is that for any value N, an optimal
solutionwilluseatmost31s,atmost33s,andatmost39sforaset
ofcoinswhosetotalisN. Thisisbecauseotherwisewecouldreplace
a set of 3 coins by a single larger coin to obtain a better solution.
This is essentially the argument why the greedy algorithm given
above will give you the smallest number of coins. The greedy pro-
cedure will start with a value N, and take as many 27s as possible
until the resulting value is less than or equal to 26. Then you move
onto the 9s, until the remaining value is less than or equal to 8, etc.
Using these observations above, the argument can be made more
preciseandmathematicallyformaltoshowthatthisgreedyalgorithm
will minimize the number of coins used.
(b) Here I give quite a detailed solution to this problem. Note that I
don’t necessarily expect you to provide pseudo-code for your pro-
posed solution. Your description should be sufficiently detailed to to
give some argument as to why your solution is correct.
Let S[n] denote the minimum number of stamps needed to make
postage for n pence. We clearly then have
S[0]=0,S[1]=1,S[2]=2,S[3]=3,S[4]=4,S[5]=5,
S[6]=6,S[7]=1,S[8]=2,S[9]=3,S[10]=1.
Thesecasesaboveshouldbeconsideredour“basecases”andwethen
work from these to compute values of S[n] for higher values of n.
The main idea, then is to consider what happens if we use a stamp
of a particular value. For example, if we want postage of n pence,
then we can clearly get it by taking one stamp of 1p, and S[n−1]
stamps (of appropriate values) to make up the remaining postage of
(n−1)p. Orifwetakea7pstamp, thenweneedS[n−7]stamps(of
the right values) to make up the rest, and similarly if we take a 10p
stamp. Thisideaisessentiallytheheartofthedynamicprogramming
algorithm that we use. The pseudo-code is given below:
Stamps(n)
(cid:3) We assume here that n≥0.
(cid:3) This procedure will determine the minimum number of stamps
of values 1p, 7p, and 10p to make up total postage of n pence.
(cid:3) Base cases
1 Set up the array S starting as above (with the
values S[i] for i=0,...,10).
2 if n≤10 then
3 return S[n]
4 else
5 for j ←11 to n do
6 S[j]=1+min{S[j−1],S[j−7],S[j−10]}
7 return S[n]
3
--- Page 4 ---
As you can see, there is one “for” loop in the body of the pseudo-
code, and this dominates the running time of the algorithm. Thus,
finding S[N] takes time which is in O(N).
Now we can find S[n] easily as above, but knowing this doesn’t tell
us the exact nature of the stamps we need. Right? It would be nice
to know that we can make 45p postage with six stamps, and that
we need one 1p, two 7p, and three 10p stamps to do so. (For some
values of n there could certainly be more than one way to do this.
For example, we can get 63p with nine 7p stamps, or with six 10p
and three 1p stamps.)
Well,wecaneasilydothistoowithanotherarraycalled,say,P. Then

tomakepostagefornp. Forexample,wewouldhaveP[45]=(1,2,3),

general, if we have P[n] = (a,b,c), then we take a 1p stamps, b
7p stamps, and c 10p stamps to make np postage. So, for example
P[7] = (0,1,0), P[10] = (0,0,1), and P[25] = (1,2,1). (As I said

to get n pence.)

The running time is still O(n).

4. Longest Increasing Subsequence. (This is a classical dynamic program-
ming problem.) Given a sequence of real numbers A ,A ,...,A , deter-
1 2 n
mine a subsequence (not necessarily contiguous) of maximum length in
which the values form a strictly increasing sequence.
For example, given the sequence of integers
−3,4,14,0,−1,3,5,2,5,10
the subsequence consisting of 4,5,10 is an increasing subsequence, as is
−3,0,3,5,10. Yourtaskistofindalongestsubsequencesothatthevalues
are strictly increasing. (There might be repeated numbers in the original
sequence, but there can be no repeats in a longest subsequence since it
must be strictly increasing.)
Hint: Define L(j) to be the length of the longest increasing subsequence
that ends at (and includes) position j. How can you find L(j) in terms
of the values L(i) for i < j? Once you know the L(j) values, what’s the
answer to the original problem? And how long does it take to compute
all of the L(j) values?
Solution: As suggested above, we define L(j) to be the length of longest
increasing subsequence that ends at (and includes) position j. If we are
able to compute these L(j) values, then the solution to our problem is
equal to L = max{L(1),L(2),...,L(n)}. Why? Because the longest
5
--- Page 6 ---
increasing subsequence must end at (and include) one of the positions
1,2,...,n.
We note that L(1) = 1, since the single value A is an “increasing” sub-
1
sequence. Also, L(2) = 1 or 2, depending upon whether A ≥ A or
1 2
A <A (recall that the sequence must be strictly increasing).
1 2
If we have computed L(1),L(2),...,L(i) for some i, how do we use these
values to find L(i+1)? We compute
L(i+1)=1+max{L(j):A <A }, or possibly L(i+1)=1 (see below).
j i+1
Inotherwords,checkallA ,findthosethatarelessthanA . Wedothis
j i+1
because if A < A , then we can increase the longest subsequence that
j i+1
endsatA byonemorevalue(namely, addingA tothatsubsequence).
j i+1
NotethatiftherearenovaluesA thatarelessthanA ,thenwedefine
j i+1
L(i+1)=1 (as for L(1)).
Computing L(i+1) requires us to check all of the values A ,...,A , i.e.
1 i
O(i)time. So,intotal,computingalloftheL(i)valuestakesO(1+2+3+
···+n)=O(n2)time. ThenfindingthevalueL=max{L(1),L(2),...,L(n)}
takes O(n) time. So finding the value L takes O(n2) time.

--- Page 1 ---
Euclid’s Algorithm and the RSA Encryption Scheme
1. For each pair of integers (a,b) below, find d=gcd(a,b) and find a pair of
numbers j and k such that d=j·a+k·b. (Use the Extended Euclidean
algorithm.)
(a) 90 and 56
(b) 91 and 89
(c) 711 and 75
(d) 815 and 75
(e) 112 and 196
(f) 366 and 150
2. For each triple of integers p,q,e given below, find the (smallest positive)
valueofdthatwillworkfortheprivatekeyoftheRSAalgorithm(where,
as usual, n=p·q and ϕ(n)=(p−1)(q−1), and ed≡1(mod ϕ(n)).
Then suppose that the message (integer) M = 25 is to be encoded using
the public key n,e pair. Find the ciphertext that corresponds to that
message M.
(a) p=7,q =13,e=5
(b) p=5,q =11,e=3
(c) p=7,q =17,e=7
3. Finally, suppose that p=5,q =17, and e=13. First find the private key
dfortheRSAmethodwiththeseparameters. Thendecrypttheciphertext
messages, C, below to find the original (plaintext) messages.
(a) 12
(b) 9
(c) 27
(d) 84
Solution:
1. First let us recall the Extended Euclidean algorithm. The pseudocode for
this method is given below:
ExtendedEuclidGCD(a,b)
Input: Nonnegative integers a and b (not both zero).
Output: d=gcd(a,b), integers j,k where d=j·a+k·b.
1 if b=0 then
2 return (a,1,0)
3 r ←a mod b
4 Let q be the integer such that a=q·b+r (that is, q =⌊a⌋).
b
5 (d,j,k)←ExtendedEuclidGCD(b,r)
6 return (d,k,j−kq)
1
--- Page 2 ---
WecanusethisalgorithmforeachpairofnumbersinQuestion1. Asthis
is a recursive procedure, we fill in the table below from left to right for
therowsinvolvinga,b,q, andr, andthenwefillitinfromrighttoleftfor
the rows involving j and k. We will assume that a≥b (as will always be
the case after the first step of the algorithm, so let’s just assume this is
the case).
a 90 56 34 22 12 10 2
b 56 34 22 12 10 2 0
q 1 1 1 1 1 5 -
(a)
r 34 22 12 10 2 0 -
j 1
k 0
We’re first established that gcd(90,56)=2.
Havingmadetherecursivefunctioncalls,we’renowabletofillinthe
values of j and k from right to left. The last column (as shown)will
always be 1 and 0 (assuming that a and b are not both 0, of course).
To get the values of j and k in a column, we use the values of j and
k from the previous column (the one to its right, as we’re filling in
the table from right to left) and the value of q that is in the same
column that we’re currently filling in.
So the final table looks like this:
a 90 56 34 22 12 10 2
b 56 34 22 12 10 2 0
q 1 1 1 1 1 5 -
r 34 22 12 10 2 0 -
j 5 -3 2 -1 1 0 1

Thus we have gcd(90,56)=2 and 2=(5)·90+(−8)·56.

2. For these problems to find d, we can again use the Extended Euclidean
algorithm. The main idea is that given e and ϕ(n) (where gcd(e,ϕ(n))=
1), we can first find the integers j and k such that j·ϕ(n)+k·e=1.
Then having found j and k, we can reduce this equation modulo ϕ(n), so
[j·ϕ(n)+k·e] (mod ϕ(n))≡1 (mod ϕ(n)).
But we have
[j·ϕ(n)+k·e] (mod ϕ(n))≡j·ϕ(n) (mod ϕ(n))+k·e (mod ϕ(n)),
and
j·ϕ(n)≡0 (mod ϕ(n)) as j·ϕ(n) is divisible by ϕ(n).
3
--- Page 4 ---
Thus we conclude that k·e≡1(mod ϕ(n)).
Now if k > 0 then we can take d = k for the RSA decryption method.
However,ifk <0,thenwewilladdamultipleofϕ(n)tok untilweobtain
a positive number and this will be the value of d that we use. Some
examples should help to clear this up.
(a) p=7,q =13,e=5
In this case we have n=p·q =91 and ϕ(91)=(p−1)(q−1)=72.
Then we use the Extended Euclidean algorithm.
a 72 5 2 1
b 5 2 1 0
q 14 2 2 -
r 2 1 0 -
j -2 1 0 1
k 29 -2 1 0
So we have (−2)·72+(29)·5=1 implying that 29·5≡1(mod 72).
In this case we can take d=29.
Then we want to compute 255 mod 91 to encode the given message.
First we find that 252 mod 91=625 mod 91=79. Then
254 mod 91=792 mod 91=6241 mod 91=53.
Finally 255 mod 91 = 53·25 mod 91 = 1325 mod 91 = 51. So the
ciphertext message that corresponds to M =25 is C =51.
(You could check that 5129 mod 91 = 25 but you’d want to use the
“fast exponentiation algorithm” that we talked about in class to do
this by hand.)
(b) p=5,q =11,e=3
We do the same as before, with n=55 and ϕ(55)=40.
a 40 3 1
b 3 1 0
q 13 3 -
r 1 0 -
j 1 0 1
k -13 1 0
This time we have that (1)·40+(−13)·3 = 1. This means that
(−13)·3≡1(mod 40).
Using what we said earlier, to get our value of d, we will add a
multiple of 40 to the number −13 until we get a positive number.
Namely, we take d=−13+40=27.
Note that we still have the true congruence 27·3≡1(mod 40) since
27·3−1=80 is a multiple of 40.
To encode the message M = 25 we want to find 253 mod 55. Then
252 mod 55=20, and so 253 mod 55=25·20 mod 55=5.
4
--- Page 5 ---
ThemessageM =25isencodedastheciphertextC =5inthiscase.
(c) p=7,q =17,e=7
The procedure is clear now (I hope!). With n = 7·17 = 119 and
ϕ(119)=6·16=96, we first find d.
a 96 7 5 2 1
b 7 5 2 1 0
q 13 1 2 2 -
r 5 2 1 0 -
j 3 -2 1 0 1
k -41 3 -2 1 0
So as usual we have (3)·96+(−41)·7=1.
Thus (−41)·7≡1(mod 96). So we take d=−41+96=55.
The message M =25 is encrypted as C =257 mod 119=32.
3. Here we have p = 5,q = 17,e = 13. Thus n = 5·17 = 85 and ϕ(85) =
4 · 16 = 64. You know the drill by now to find the decryption key d.
a 64 13 12 1
b 13 12 1 0
q 4 1 12 -
r 12 1 0 -
j -1 1 0 1
k 5 -1 1 0
So (−1)·64+(5)·13 = 1 and therefore 5·13 ≡ 1(mod 64). In this case
we have d=5.
Using this decryption key, we can decode the messages that we’re given
by finding Cd mod 85=C5 mod 85.
(a) 125 mod 85=37.
(b) 95 mod 85=59.
(c) 275 mod 85=57.
(d) 845 mod 85=84.
(So 84 is “decoded” as the number 84, and hence, 84 is also encoded
as 84.)
For all of these numbers, we can also check that M13 mod 85 = C.
For example, we have 3713 mod 85 = 12 (a small verification that
the RSA scheme works as intended).

Approximation Algorithms
■ Many optimization problems are NP-hard!
(For example, the traveling salesperson problem.)
⇝
■ an optimal solution cannot be efficiently computed
unless P=NP.
■ However, good approximate solutions can often be found
efficiently!
■ Will see two examples of such approximation algorithms for
the Vertex Cover and Steiner Tree problems, respectively.

Approximation Alg. for
Algorithm VertexCover(G )
M ← ∅
foreach e ∈ E (G ) do
if e is not adjacent to any edge in M then
M ← M ∪ {e}
return { u, v | uv ∈ M }
Theorem. The above algorithm is a factor-2 approximation
VertexCover
algorithm for .
□
Proof. ALG = 2 · |M| ≤ 2 · OPT

SteinerTree
Restriction of where the graph G is complete
(i.e., has an edge uv for every pair of distinct vertices u, v )
and the cost function is metric, i.e., for every triple u, v , w of
vertices, we have c(u, w ) ≤ c(u, v ) + c(v , w ).

SteinerTree
2-Approximation for
MetricSteinerTree
Theorem. For an instance of ,
let B be a minimum spanning tree (MST) of the
subgraph G [T ] induced by the terminal set T .
Then c(B) ≤ 2 · OPT.
1
2 1 2
3 2 2
4 4

--- Page 19 ---
Proof of Approximation Factor
Consider an optimal Steiner tree B∗.
Duplicate all edges of B∗. ⇒ graph B′ with cost c(B′) = 2 · OPT.
Find tour T ′ in B′ traversing every edge exactly once (DFS in B∗)
⇒ c(T ′) = c(B′) = 2 · OPT
Find path H in G[T ] that visits all terminals by “short-cutting” Steiner
vertices and previously visited terminals.
⇒ c(H) ≤ c(T ′) = 2 · OPT since G is metric.
MST B of G[T ] costs c(B) ≤ c(H) ≤ 2 · OPT
since H is a spanning tree of G[T ].
′
T
B ∗ H

Outlook
Today, approximation algorithms are known for hundreds of optimisation
problems arising in various areas of application such as logististics,
network design, scheduling, data mining, and machine learning.
It is a vibrant research area with many beautiful ideas in algorithms
design and analysis
If you are interested in this topic, for example, as part of your third-year
project then please contact me (Joachim Spoerhase;
joachim.spoerhase@liverpool.ac.uk).

Module Outcomes
Goal of module:
To train you to think algorithmically, to be able to analyze the
correctness of algorithms and their performance in terms of the use
of time and space, and to be able to think critically about
algorithmics in general.
From Wikipedia: Algorithmics is the systematic study of the design
and analysis of algorithms. This course is among the most useful
courses to give you knowledge that helps developing your superb
programming skills!

--- Page 16 ---
What is an algorithm?
An algorithm is a sequence of steps for performing a task in a
finite amount of time.
What algorithms do you already know?
16/55
--- Page 17 ---
What is an algorithm? (cont.)
For computational algorithms, we use data structures to store,
access, and manipulate data.
What data structures have you encountered in the past?
Our fundamental interest is to design “good” algorithms which
utilize efficient data structures. In this context “good” (typically)
means fast in a way to be clarified during this course.
17/55
--- Page 18 ---
A first simple example
Suppose you have a list of some type (integers, names, etc.), and
you want to search through the list to see if some element is
already in the list.
How can you do this search? Moreover, how can you do it
efficiently?
Relevant questions
How is the list stored?
Is it sorted or not?
If the list is sorted, it’s (often) easier to perform the search.
If it’s not sorted, should we sort it first?
Are we going to be doing this search operation many times?
(Then we might want to sort the list first, if necessary.)
Are we going to be updating the list (i.e. adding and/or
deleting items)?
18/55
--- Page 19 ---
Algorithm Analysis
Primary interest: Running time (time-complexity) of the
algorithm and the operations on data structures.
Secondary interest: Space (or “memory”) usage
(space-complexity).
Also of interest: (Near) Optimality of the output solution
(for optimisation problems).
This module focuses on the mathematical design and analysis of
algorithms and their associated data structures, utilizing relevant
mathematical tools to do so.
Let’s first discuss the difference between experimental analysis and
theoretical analysis of algorithms.
19/55
--- Page 20 ---
Experimental Analysis of Algorithms
We are interested in dependency of the running time on size of
the input.
The size could mean the number of vertices and edges if we are
operating on a graph, the length of a message we’re
encoding/decoding, and/or the actual length of numbers we’re
processing in terms of the bits needed to store them in memory.
To analyze algorithms we might perform experiments to empirically
observe the running time.
Doing so requires a good choice of sample inputs, and an
appropriate number of tests (so that we can have statistical
certainty about our analysis).
20/55
--- Page 21 ---
Experimental Analysis (cont.)
Also, the running time depends on both the size and instance of
input and the algorithm used, as well as the software and hardware
environment on which it is run.
input size
emit
gninnur
input size
emit
gninnur
21/55
--- Page 22 ---
Drawbacks of Experimental Analysis
1 Experiments are performed on a limited set of test inputs.
2 Requires all tests to be performed using same hardware and
software.
3 Requires implementation and execution of algorithm.
22/55
--- Page 23 ---
Theoretical Analysis
Benefits over experimental analysis:
1 Can take all possible inputs into account.
2 Can compare efficiency of two (or more) algorithms,
independent of hardware/software environment.
3 Involves studying high-level descriptions of algorithms (e.g.
pseudo-code).
23/55
--- Page 24 ---
Theoretical Analysis (cont.)
When we analyze an algorithm in this fashion we aim to
associate a function f(n) to each algorithm, where f(n)
characterizes the running-time in terms of some measure of
the input-size, n.
(For example, n could be the number of items to be sorted in
a list.)
Typical functions include: n,logn,n2,nlogn,n5,2n,...
24/55
--- Page 25 ---
Theoretical Analysis (requirements)
For formal theoretical analysis, we need:
1 A language to describe algorithms.
2 Computational model in which algorithms are executed.
3 Metric for measuring running-time.
4 A way of characterizing running-time.
25/55
--- Page 26 ---
Pseudo-code
Pseudo-code is a high-level description language for algorithms.
Psuedo-code provides more structured description of algorithms.
Allows high-level analysis of algorithms to determine their running
time (and space requirements).
26/55
--- Page 27 ---
Pseudo-code example
Finding the minimum element of a set of numbers, stored in an
array A.
Minimum-Element(A)
/** Find the minimum of elements in the array A. **/
1 min ← A[1]
2 for j ← 2 to length[A]
3 do
4 (cid:3) Compare element A[j] to current min
5 (cid:3) and store it if it’s smaller.
6 if A[j] < min
7 then min ← A[j]
8 return min
27/55
--- Page 28 ---
Pseudo-code (cont.)
Pseudo-code is a mixture of natural language and high-level
programming language (e.g., Java, Python, C, etc.).
Describes a generic implementation of data structures or
algorithms.
Pseudo-code includes: expressions, declarations, variable
initialization and assignments, conditionals, loops, arrays,
method calls, etc.
I won’t formally define a strict method for giving pseudo-code, but
when using it we aim to describe an algorithm in a manner that
would allow a competent programmer to translate the pseudo-code
into program code without misinterpretation of the algorithm.
28/55
--- Page 29 ---
Correctness
Loop invariants are useful to show correctness of an algorithm given
as pseudo-code.
In this case, a useful loop invariant is “at the start of the j’th
iteration of the for loop of Lines 2-7, min equals the minimum
value in A[1..j −1].”
29/55
--- Page 30 ---
Loop invariants
Three essential things to show for loop invariants.
Initialisation: It is true prior to the first iteration of the loop.
Maintenance: If it is true before an iteration of the loop, it
remains true before the next iteration.
Termination: When the loop terminates, the invariant gives
us a useful property that helps show the algorithm correct.
Additional reading: Chapter 2.1, 2.2 of CLRS for pseudo-code and
analysis of the slightly more complicated algorithm of Insertion Sort.
30/55
--- Page 31 ---
Computational Model
Our computational model is like that typically found on modern-day
computers.
CPU connected to a bank of memory cells.
Each memory cell can store a number, character, or address.
We use a set of high-level primitive operations: assignment,
method invocation, arithmetic operations, comparison of numbers,
array indexing, returning from a method, etc.
31/55
--- Page 32 ---
Computational Model (cont.)
Assumption: primitive operations (like a single addition,
multiplication, or comparison) require constant time to perform.
(Not necessarily true, e.g. on modern processors, multiplication
generally takes about three to four times as long to perform on a
computer than addition. Division typically takes at least twice as
long as multiplication.)
When we analyze the running time of an algorithm we count
the number of operations executed during the course of the
algorithm.
32/55
--- Page 33 ---
Counting Primitive Operations
Minimum-Element(A)
1 min ← A[1]
2 for j ← 2 to length[A]
3 do
4 (cid:3) Compare element A[j] to current min
5 (cid:3) and store it if it’s smaller.
6 if A[j] < min
7 then min ← A[j]
8 return min
How many primitive operations?
1 One assignment in line 1;
2 length[A]−1 more assignments (to j) in line 2;
3 Line 6 gives length[A]−1 comparisons;
4 A possible length[A]−1 more assignments from line 7;
5 Finally there’s the last statement to execute once;
⇒ A maximum of 3(length[A]−1)+2 primitive operations.
33/55
--- Page 34 ---
Average- vs. Worst-Case Complexity
An algorithm may run faster on some inputs compared to others.
Average-case complexity refers to running time as an average
taken over all inputs of the same size.
Worst-case complexity refers to running time as the maximum
taken over all inputs of the same size.
Usually, we’re most interested in worst-case complexity.
34/55
--- Page 35 ---
Average- vs. Worst-Case Complexity (cont.)
5 Worst case
4
Average case
3
Best case
2
1
A B C D E F

emiT
gninnuR
35/55
--- Page 36 ---
Asymptotic notation
Asymptotic notation allows characterization of the main
factors affecting running time.
Used in a simplified analysis that estimates the number of primitive
operations executed up to a constant factor.
Such notation lets us compare the running times of two algorithms.
You first encountered this type of notation in COMP108.
36/55

“Big-Oh” Notation
“Big-Oh” notation is probably the most commonly used form of
asymptotic notation.
Given two positive functions f(n) and g(n) (defined on the
nonnegative integers), we say f(n) is O(g(n)), written
f(n) ∈ O(g(n)), if there are constants c and n such that:
0
f(n) ≤ c ·g(n) for all n ≥ n .

Further examples of Big-Oh
1 3n2+n+16 is O(n2). Why?
We know that n ≤ n2 for all n ≥ 1. Also, 16 ≤ n2 for n ≥ 4.
So

Ω(n) and Θ(n) notation
We say that f(n) is Ω(g(n)) (big-Omega) if there are real
constants c and n such that:
0
f(n) ≥ cg(n) for all n ≥ n .
0
We say that f(n) is Θ(g(n)) (Theta) if f(n) is Ω(g(n)) and
f(n) is also O(g(n)). (Equivalently, g(n) is O(f(n)) and f(n)
is O(g(n)).)

--- Page 45 ---
Back to “finding the minimum”
We previously said that if an array A has n items, then the running
time of our “minimum finding algorithm” takes 3n−1 primitive
operations.
Thus, this is an algorithm that is O(n).
However, this is best possible, since we have to examine each item
in the list to really determine the minimum. (So it’s a Ω(n)
algorithm.)
Putting these observations together, our minimum finding
algorithm on a list of n elements is a Θ(n) algorithm.

--- Page 48 ---
NP-completeness (a brief mention for now)
There are certain problems whose exact solutions seem hard to
find, i.e. solving large instances of them could take decades or
centuries of computing time since it appears we have to try
(nearly) all of the exponentially many possible solutions.
Many of these are, however, important problems that business
and industry (airlines, manufacturers, telecommunications
companies, etc.) are interested in solving, such as some
scheduling and optimization problems. This “hardness” can
also be described in terms of the game Minesweeper, the very
one included with the Windows OS.
In the absence of exact solutions, good approximations are used, we
will see examples of these towards the end of this module.
48/55
--- Page 49 ---
NP-completeness (cont.)
Certain problems, the so-called class of NP-complete problems, are
the “hardest” ones.
Somewhat surprisingly, if any one of these special problems can be
solved efficiently, then all of them can be solved efficiently. So
these problems are equivalent in a formal computational sense.
We will examine NP-completeness in closer detail later on in the
course.
49/55
--- Page 50 ---
NP-completeness (cont.)
The class P: problems that are efficiently solvable.
The class NP: problems that are efficiently verifiable.
Is P = NP?
This is such an important and fundamental problem that it’s been
named as one of the seven “Millennium Problems” by the Clay
Institute and can earn you $1 million dollars for its solution (and a
place in mathematical and computer science history).
Go to the website
https://www.claymath.org/millennium/p-vs-np/
to read more.
50/55
--- Page 51 ---
Simple Justification Techniques
Here’s some proof techniques that we will likely use in this course.
Proof by mathematical induction.
Proof by contradiction.
Proof by contrapositive argument.
(Dis)Proof by counterexample.

Examples of results provable by induction
n(n+1)

2

6
3 2n ≥ n2 for n ≥ 5.
Outline of a proof by induction:
Base case: Show hypothesis true for smallest value of n.
Inductive step: assume hypothesis to be true for n = k and
show it true for n = k +1.
Example: first bullet above:
k(k+1) (k+1)(k+2)
1+2+···+k +(k +1) = +k +1 = .
2 2
52/55
--- Page 53 ---
Proof by contradiction
Proposition
If ab is odd and a,b are natural numbers, then a is odd or b is even.
Proof: Suppose the proposition is not true. Then, a is even AND
b is odd (DeMorgan’s Law).
Thus a = 2i for some integer i.
Hence ab = (2i)b = (2i)b, which is even.
But ab cannot be simultaneously odd and even (this is a
contradiction).
53/55
--- Page 54 ---
Proof by contrapositive
Statement 1: If it is raining, I am not playing football.
Statement 2: If I am playing football, it is not raining.
Proposition
If ab is odd and a,b are natural numbers, then a is odd or b is even.
Note that p =⇒ q is equivalent to ¬q =⇒ ¬p. Proof: The
contrapositive is ‘If a is even and b is odd, then ab is even.’
We will prove the contrapositive. So, suppose a = 2i for some
integer i. Then ab = (2i)b = 2(ib), which is even.
54/55
--- Page 55 ---
(Dis)Proof by counterexample
Claim
If i > 1 is an integer, then 2i −1 is a prime.
(Dis)Proof: Consider i = 4. We have 24−1 = 15 = 3·5.
55/55

COMP202
Complexity of Algorithms

1/39
--- Page 2 ---
Learning Outcomes
At the conclusion of this set of lecture notes, you should:
1 Understand the general idea of Divide-and-Conquer algorithms.
2 Be familiar with some of the classical Divide-and-Conquer
algorithms.
3 Be able to utilize the “Master Method” that may be used to
derive closed form expressions for (some) recurrence relations.
2/39
--- Page 3 ---
Sorting
Sorting problem: Given a collection C of n elements (and a total
ordering), arrange the elements of C into non-decreasing order, e.g.,
[45,3,67,1,5,16,105,8]
[1,3,5,8,16,45,67,105]
MergeSort, which we will now see, is a classical Divide-and-Conquer
algorithm.
3/39
--- Page 4 ---
Incremental algorithms
We saw an algorithm for minimum finding last week, this was an
incremental algorithm: having stored the minimum of A[1..j −1],
the next step finds and stores the minimum of A[1..j].
4/39
--- Page 5 ---
Divide-and-Conquer
The divide-and-conquer method is a means that can be used to
solve some algorithmic problems. This general method consists of
the following steps:
Divide: If the input size is small then solve the problem
directly; otherwise, divide the input data into two or more
subsets, typically disjoint.
Recur: Recursively solve the sub-problems associated with
subsets.
Conquer: Take the solutions to sub-problems and merge into a
solution to the original problem.
5/39
--- Page 6 ---
MergeSort
MergeSort is one way we can apply the divide-and-conquer method
to perform sorting. The MergeSort method consists of the
following steps:
Divide: If input sequence S has 0 or 1 elements, then return S;
otherwise, split S into two sequences S and S , each
1 2
containing about 1 of the elements in S.
2
Recur: Recursively sort S and S .
1 2
Conquer: Put the elements back into S by merging the sorted
sequences S and S into a single sorted sequence.
1 2
6/39
--- Page 7 ---
MergeSort: Pseudocode
7/39
--- Page 8 ---
MergeSort - Illustration
1. Divide in half
Split list equally
2. Recur 2. Recur
S S
1 2
3. Merge

--- Page 9 ---
MergeSort - Example
30 26 41 3 9 63 12

9/39
--- Page 10 ---
MergeSort - Analysis
Theorem: Merging two sorted sequences S and S takes
1 2
O(n +n ) time, where n is the size of S and n is the size of S .
1 2 1 1 2 2
Theorem: MergeSort runs in O(nlogn) time in the worst (and
average) case.
Idea: During every recursive step, dividing each sublist takes time
at most O(n) time. Merging all of the lists in each level takes at
most O(n) time as well.
How many times do we recurse? This is at most O(logn) times.
Hence this gives the O(nlogn) running time for MergeSort.
10/39
--- Page 11 ---
MergeSort - Analysis
height time per level
n O(n)
n/2 n/2 O(n)
O(logn)
n/4 n/4 n/4 n/4 O(n)
Total time: O(nlog n)
11/39
--- Page 12 ---
Divide-and-Conquer (cont.)
To analyze the running time of a Divide-and-Conquer algorithm we
typically utilize a recurrence relation, where
T(n) denotes the worst-possible running time on an input of
size n.
We then want to characterize T(n) using an equation that relates
T(n) to values of function T for problem sizes smaller than n.
12/39
--- Page 13 ---
MergeSort recurrence
For example, the running time of the MergeSort algorithm can be
written in this form:
(cid:26)
c if n < 2
T(n) =
2T(n/2)+dn otherwise
where c,d are small constants that represents how much work is
done for the comparisons for merging the lists, etc.
(Note: Strictly speaking this should be written as
(cid:26)
c if n < 2
T(n) =
T(⌊n/2⌋)+T(⌈n/2⌉)+dn otherwise
but in terms of the asymptotic running time, the “floors” and
“ceilings” are unnecessary, and this can be formally proven.)
13/39
--- Page 14 ---
Binary search recurrence
For another example, the running time of the binary search method
(on sorted arrays) can be described as
(cid:26)
c if n < 2
T(n) =
T(n/2)+c otherwise
where the constant c here represents the work needed to determine
the midpoint of the sublist and do the comparison to determine if
the sought for value has been found (or updating the bottom/top
index to determine the next sublist to search).
14/39
--- Page 15 ---
Substitution Method
One way to solve a Divide-and-Conquer recurrence equation is to
use the iterative substitution method, a.k.a. “plug-and-chug”, e.g.
(cid:26)
c if n < 2
T(n) =
2T(n/2)+cn otherwise.
Assuming that n is a power of 2 (for simplicity) we get
T(n) = 2 (cid:2) 2T(n/22)+c(n/2) (cid:3) +cn = 22T(n/22)+2cn.
After j iterations we have
T(n) = 2jT(n/2j)+jcn.
For j = log n, we get
2
T(n) = 2log 2 n ·c +cnlog n = cn+cnlog n = Θ(nlogn).
2 2
15/39
--- Page 16 ---
Maximum-subarray problem
Say you want to invest in a company’s stock. You are allowed to
buy one unit of the stock on a day of your choosing, and sell it at a
later day of your choosing.
Say you also have the power to see future stock prices. Goal is to
maximise profit (sell price - buy price).
Price = [100,113,110,85,105,102,86,63,81,101,94,106,101]
Change = [13,−3,−25,20,−3,16,−23,18,20,−7,12,−5]
Brute force: Try every possible buy and sell dates in which buy
precedes sell date. A period of n days has
(cid:0)n(cid:1)
such dates. Since
2
(cid:0)n(cid:1) = Θ(n2), and the evaluation of each pair of dates can take
2
O(1) time at best, this approach takes Ω(n2) time.
16/39
--- Page 17 ---
Divide-and-conquer solution
Suppose we want to find a maximum subarray of the subarray
A[low..high]. Let mid be the midpoint of the array. Any
contiguous subarray A[i..j] of A[low..high] must lie in exactly one
of the following places:
entirely in A[low..mid], i.e., low ≤ i ≤ j ≤ mid,
entirely in A[mid +1..high], i.e., mid < i ≤ j ≤ high, or
crossing the midpoint, i.e., low ≤ mid ≤ j ≤ high.
Will find maximum subarray values of each of these three: the first
two recursively because they are smaller instances of the original
problem. For the third, can find maximum subarrays of the form
A[i..mid] and A[mid +1..j] in linear time and combine them.
See pseudo-code in Section 4.1 in CLRS!
17/39
--- Page 18 ---
Analysis
As in MergeSort, let T(n) denote running time of this algorithm on
an array of size n.
Clearly, T(1) = Θ(1). The first two bullets on the previous slide
contribute to 2T(n/2), and the third to cn for a constant c. Thus,
(cid:40)
Θ(n) n = 1
T(n) =
2T(n/2)+cn n > 1
This is the same recurrence as MergeSort! So the running time of
this algorithm is O(nlogn).
18/39
--- Page 19 ---
Matrix Multiplication: Another example
Suppose we are given two n×n matrices X and Y, and we wish to
compute their product Z = X ·Y, which is defined in this manner:
n
(cid:88)
Z[i,j] = X[i,k]·Y[k,j].
k=1
Each element Z[i,j] takes time Θ(n) to compute (since there are n
multiplications and n−1 additions to perform), assuming that any
multiplication or addition takes constant time.
As there are n2 elements to compute for Z, this naturally leads to
an Θ(n3) time algorithm.
See pseudocode in Section 4.2, CLRS!

Another useful way of viewing this product (especially for large

(cid:18) (cid:19) (cid:18) (cid:19)(cid:18) (cid:19)

Strassen’s Algorithm for matrix multiplication
Volker Strassen (in 1969) realized that matrix multiplication can be
performed using a divide and conquer approach that uses a smaller
number of multiplications.
First define the following seven matrix products:
S = A(F −H)
1
S = (A+B)H
2
S = (C +D)E
3
S = D(G −E)
4
S = (A+D)(E +H)
5
S = (B −D)(G +H)
6
S = (C −A)(E +F)
7
22/39
--- Page 23 ---
Strassen’s Algorithm (cont.)
We can then find I,J,K, and L using the S ’s as follows:
i
I = S +S +S −S
4 5 6 2
J = S +S
1 2
K = S +S
3 4
L = S +S +S −S
1 5 7 3
23/39
--- Page 24 ---
Strassen’s Algorithm (cont.)
Thus, we can compute Z = X ·Y using only seven recursive
multiplications of matrices of size n/2×n/2.
Therefore, the running time of Strassen’s Algorithm satisfies this
recursive relation:
T(n) = 7T(n/2)+bn2.
Using this recursive formula, together with the Master Method
(which we’ll see soon), we obtain:
Theorem: We can multiply two n×n matrices in O(nlog 2 7) =
O(n2.808) time.
24/39
--- Page 25 ---
Divide-and-Conquer style recurrence relations
We saw an example of using the Substitution method
(“plug-and-chug”) to solve the recurrence for MergeSort.
We might also “guess” a solution to a divide-and-conquer style
recurrence relation, and then formally prove this using the method
of mathematical induction.
Let us consider the more general case, where
(cid:26)
c if n ≤ d
T(n) =
aT(n/b)+f(n) if n > d
for some constants a,b,c, and d, and some function f(n).
25/39
--- Page 26 ---
Divide-and-Conquer style recurrence relations (cont.)
We can “unwind” this recurrence relation in this manner:
T(n) = aT(n/b)+f(n)
= a (cid:2) aT(n/b2)+f(n/b) (cid:3) +f(n)
= a2T(n/b2)+af(n/b)+f(n)
= a3T(n/b3)+a2f(n/b2)+af(n/b)+f(n)
= a4T(n/b4)+a3f(n/b3)+a2f(n)/b2)+af(n/b)+f(n)
= ...
log n−1
(cid:88)b
= alog b nT(1)+ aj f(n/bj)
j=0
log n−1
(cid:88)b
= nlog b aT(1)+ aj f(n/bj).
j=0
(We use alog b n = nlog b a to get the final line.)
26/39
--- Page 27 ---
Recurrence tree for T(n) = 3T(n/4) + cn2
27/39
--- Page 28 ---
Divide-and-Conquer style recurrence relations (cont.)
With a careful analysis of the previous expression, we can make
some conclusions about the (asymptotic) form of T(n).
For example, if f(n) is “smaller” than nlog b a (in a suitable fashion),
then the first term is going to dominate the summation, and we will
have T(n) = Θ(nlog b a).
If, on the other hand, we have nlog b a is “smaller” than f(n) (and
the function f is “nice”), then the summation will dominate this
expression and we will have T(n) = Θ(f(n)).
And there’s a third case to consider too...
28/39
--- Page 29 ---
The Master Method
We make the following claim:
Theorem
Suppose that T(n) and f(n) satisfy the recurrence relation defined
previously (for some constants a,b,δ, and d).
Then
1 If there is a constant ε > 0 such that f(n) is O(nlog b a−ε),
then T(n) is Θ(nlog b a).
2 If there is a constant k ≥ 0 such that f(n) is Θ(nlog b alogkn),
then T(n) is Θ(nlog b alogk+1n).
3 If there are constants ε > 0 and δ < 1 such that f(n) is
Ω(nlog b a+ε) and af(n/b) ≤ δf(n) for n ≥ d, then T(n) is
Θ(f(n)).
29/39
--- Page 30 ---
Examples using the Master Method
Consider these examples:
1 T(n) = 4T(n/2)+n.
In this case we have nlog b a = nlog 2 4 = n2. Therefore we are in
Case 1, since f(n) = n = O(n2−ε) for ε = 1. Thus, we
conclude that T(n) is Θ(n2) by the Master Method.
2 T(n) = 2T(n/2)+cn. (This is the MergeSort type of
recurrence relation.)
Here we have nlog b a = nlog 2 2 = n1 = n. Hence we are in Case
2 with k = 0 (why?), so that T(n) is Θ(nlogn).
3 T(n) = 2T(n/2)+nlogn.
As before, we have log 2 2 = 1, so nlog b a = n. We are also in
Case 2, with k = 1 as f(n) is Θ(nlogn).
Therefore, we conclude T(n) is Θ(nlog2n).
30/39
--- Page 31 ---
Examples using the Master Method (cont.)
4. T(n) = 9T(n/3)+n2.5.
In this case we have nlog b a = nlog 3 9 = n2.
We are in Case 3, since f(n) = n2.5 = Ω(n2+ε), for ε = 1/2,
and af(n/b) = 9(n/3)2.5 = (1/3)1/2f(n). (So δ = (1/3)1/2
works for the statement of Case 3.) This means that T(n) is
Θ(n2.5) by the Master Method.
5. T(n) = 7T(n/3)+n.
Here we see that nlog b a = nlog 3 7. Since 1 < log 3 7, we see
that there is some (small) ε > 0 such that 1 < log 7−ε and
3
therefore f(n) = n = O(nlog 3 7−ε). Hence, we see that T(n) is
Θ(nlog
3
7).
31/39
--- Page 32 ---
Example: Variable Change
6. Variable change: T(n) = 2T(n1/2)+logn.
Unfortunately this form doesn’t allow us to use the master
method.
Fix: Set k = logn.
T(n) = T(2k) = 2T(2k/2)+k.
Substituting into S(k) = T(2k), we get
S(k) = 2S(k/2)+k.
Master method gives S(k) = O(klogk). Substituting back for
T(n) implies T(n) = O(lognloglogn).
32/39
--- Page 33 ---
Fast multiplication of integers
Now we consider a Divide-and-Conquer method to multiply integers
(especially when the integers are very large, such as those used in
various encryption schemes).
Suppose that I and J are two integers with n binary digits each.
We can easily find I +J and I −J in time Θ(n). Computing the
product I ·J, using the common grade-school algorithm, take
Θ(n2) time. Can we do better? (Assuming that n is quite large...)
We will assume now that n is a power of 2 (which we can do by
padding I and J with zeroes at the beginning).
33/39
--- Page 34 ---
Fast multiplication of integers (cont.)
Taking an inspiration (maybe?) from the MergeSort algorithm, let
us consider writing I and J in two parts, namely the high-order and
low-order bits.
In other words, we can write I and J as
I = I 2n/2+I
h ℓ
J = J 2n/2+J
h ℓ
We observe that multiplying an integer by a power of 2 (like 2k) is
easy to do on a computer, as it is a (left) shift operation.
If a shift by one bit takes constant time to perform, then
multiplying by 2k takes Θ(k) time.
34/39
--- Page 35 ---
Fast multiplication of integers (cont.)
Given the representation of I and J above, in terms of the high-
and low-order bits, let’s concentrate on computing the product of I
and J.
Given the above representation, we see that
I ·J = (I 2n/2+I )·(J 2n/2+J )
h ℓ h ℓ
= I J 2n +I J 2n/2+I J 2n/2+I J
h h ℓ h h ℓ ℓ ℓ
We can use a Divide-and-Conquer type of algorithm to compute
this product.
Namely, let’s divide I and J into the high- and low-order bits as
above, compute four products of these (size n/2 bit) pieces, and
then combine them together as above (performing some shift
operations, to multiply by 2n/2 or 2n where needed).
35/39
--- Page 36 ---
Fast multiplication of integers (cont.)
Computing the shifts and additions requires, in total, time O(n) so
we end up with a recurrence relation that looks like (for n ≥ 2)
T(n) = 4T(n/2)+cn
for some constant c.
Applying the Master Method, we find we are in Case 1, namely
since nlog 2 4 = n2, and f(n) = cn = O(n2−ε) for, say, ε = 1/2, we
find that T(n) = Θ(n2).
Hmmm, so our Divide-and-Conquer method hasn’t helped us do
things any quicker...
36/39
--- Page 37 ---
Fast multiplication of integers (cont.)
The Master Method, however, gives us insight into how we could
improve our approach.
If we can reduce the number of recursive calls (by reducing the
value of a = 4), we could improve the overall running time.
So let us consider the product
(I −I )(J −J ) = I J −I J −I J +I J .
h ℓ ℓ h h ℓ h h ℓ ℓ ℓ h
This might be an odd product to consider, but note that it consists
of two of the products we want to compute, namely
I J and I J ,
h ℓ ℓ h
together with two of the products that we (will) compute
recursively, namely
I J and I J .
h h ℓ ℓ
37/39
--- Page 38 ---
Fast multiplication of integers (cont.)
So our Divide-and-Conquer strategy becomes this:
1 Write I and J in terms of their high- and low-order bits.
2 Compute (recursively) the products I h J h , I ℓ J ℓ , and
(I −I )(J −J ).
h ℓ ℓ h
3 Combine these together to get
I ·J = (I 2n/2+I )(J 2n/2+J )
h ℓ h ℓ
= I J 2n +[(I −I )(J −J )+I J +I J ]2n/2+I J
h h h ℓ ℓ h h h ℓ ℓ ℓ ℓ
= I J 2n +I J 2n/2+I J 2n/2+I J .
h h ℓ h h ℓ ℓ ℓ
The important point is that this requires only three recursive
multiplications of integers with n/2 bits.
38/39
--- Page 39 ---
Fast multiplication of integers (cont.)
The recurrence relation describing the running time to multiply the
integers now becomes
T(n) = 3T(n/2)+cn
for a constant c > 0.
Since 1 < log 3, we can find a small ε > 0 such that
2
f(n) = cn = O(nlog 2 3−ε). So we’re still in Case 1 of the Master
Method, but now we have this result:
Theorem
We can multiply two n-bit integers in time Θ(nlog 2 3) = O(n1.585).
39/39

COMP202
Complexity of Algorithms
Probabilistic analysis and randomized
algorithms
Reading materials: Appendix C.2, C.3, Chapter 7 in CLRS.
1/31
--- Page 2 ---
Learning Outcomes
At the conclusion of this set of lecture notes, you should:
1 Understand basics of probability theory.
2 Be able to explain the mechanism behind the randomised
Quick Sort algorithm, and why randomisation is used.
3 Be able to explain the mechanism behind the randomised
Select algorithm and identify key differences with the

2/31
--- Page 3 ---
Basic Probability
Probability theory is useful in many contexts in the theory
of algorithms.
It can be used to analyze the average-case performance of
algorithms (by weighting the running time according to a
probability distribution on inputs.
Randomized algorithms (those that use random bits during
their execution to make decisions) are also becoming more
commonplace and useful in recent years. Analysis of such
randomized algorithms also requires use of probability theory.
See Appendix C in [CLRS] for details on probability theory!
3/31
--- Page 4 ---
Sample space
Sample space is the set of all possible outcomes from an
experiment.
Examples:
1 Rolling a fair (6-sided) die. The sample space is
{1,2,3,4,5,6}.
2 Flipping a fair coin. The sample space is {H,T}.
3 Flipping a fair coin until it comes up heads. Here the
sample space is infinite, with the ith possible outcome
being a sequence of i −1 tails followed by a single heads,
for i = 1,2,3,4,...
4/31
--- Page 5 ---
Probability spaces, Events
A Probability space is a sample space S and a probability
function Pr, which maps subsets of S to [0,1].
Each subset, A ⊆ S, is called an event.
5/31
--- Page 6 ---
Probability function
A probability function, Pr, possesses the following properties:
1 Pr(∅) = 0,
2 Pr(S) = 1,
3 0 ≤ Pr(A) ≤ 1, for any A ⊆ S,
4 If A,B ⊆ S and A∩B = ∅, then
Pr(A∪B) = Pr(A)+Pr(B).
6/31
--- Page 7 ---
An example of a probability space
Consider the sample space earlier, namely that of flipping a fair
coin until it comes up heads.
We can represent the sample space as an infinite set of strings,
namely {H,TH,TTH,TTTH,...}.
The probability function on this sample space assigns these
probabilities to events:
Pr(H) = 1/2
Pr(TH) = 1/4
Pr(TTH) = 1/8
...
Pr(H or TTH) = 1/2+1/8 = 5/8
...
(These statements make use of the independence of coin flips
discussed on the next slide, and the last statement uses the
final property of the probability function on the previous slide.)
7/31
--- Page 8 ---
Independent Events
Two events are independent if Pr(A∩B) = Pr(A)·Pr(B).
A collection of events {A ,A ,...,A } is mutually
1 2 n
independent if
Pr(A ∩A ∩...∩A ) = Pr(A )·Pr(A )·...·Pr(A )
i1 i2 ik i1 i2 ik
for any subcollection of events {A ,...,A }.
i1 ik
8/31
--- Page 9 ---
Example
Let A be the event that the roll of a fair die is 6, and let B be the
event that the roll of a second fair die is a 3, and let C be the
event that the sum of the two dice is 10.
Then A and B are independent events, but it’s easy to check
that C is not independent with either A or B.
This follows since
Pr(A) = Pr(B) = 1/6
Pr(C) = 3/36
Pr(A∩C) = 1/36 = Pr(A∩B),and
Pr(B∩C) = 0.
9/31
--- Page 10 ---
Conditional Probability
The conditional probability of an event A occurring, given
another event B, denoted Pr(A|B) is
Pr(A|B) = Pr(A∩B)/Pr(B), assuming that Pr(B) > 0.
Inotherwords,conditionalprobabilitytalksabouttheprobability
of some event occurring, given the knowledge that some other
event has already occurred.
10/31
--- Page 11 ---
Examples
1 Aiseventthattwothrowsofafairdiesumto10,B isevent
that the first throw is 6.
Pr(B) = 1/6 and Pr(A∩B) = 1/36.
Thus, Pr(A|B) = (1/36)/(1/6) = 1/6.
2 Suppose that you have a red die and a black die.
LetC betheeventthatafterthrowingthetwodicetheysum
to 9, and D be the event that the red die shows a 5 or 6.
Then (as you can verify, think about it!!!):
Pr(D) = 2/6
Pr(C ∩D) = 2/36
Hence,
Pr(C|D) = Pr(C ∩D)/Pr(D) = (2/36)/(2/6) = 1/6.
Also, Pr(C) = 4/36. Hence,
Pr(D|C) = Pr(D∩C)/Pr(C) = (2/36)/(4/36) = 1/2.

Random variables
A random variable is a function X that maps outcomes
from some sample space to real numbers.
In algorithm analysis we often use a random variable X with a
discrete set of outcomes to characterize the running time of a
randomized algorithm.
The expected value of a discrete random variable X is
definedasE(X) = (cid:80) i ·Pr(X = i)wherethesummationis
i
over the range of possible values of X.
The expected value is linear, meaning that for any two random
variables X and Y we have E(X +Y) = E(X)+E(Y).
12/31

Another way to get the same result
We actually don’t need to use calculus for this. If you consider
the first coin toss, either it comes up heads (in which case the
experiment is over), or it doesn’t (which “costs” us one coin
toss, plus however many more we need until the first heads
shows up).
Thus we can write
E(X) = Pr(X isheads)·1+
Pr(X istails)·(1+expectednumberofadditionalcoin
tossesuntilfirstheadsappears).
But the “expected number of additional coin tosses until the first
heads appears” is exactly the same as E(X). (Having first
obtained a “tails” the experiment “resets itself” and we’re just
interested in obtaining the first heads again.)

QuickSort
The QuickSort algorithm is a divide-and-conquer method for
performing sorting. This method differs from MergeSort in that
the steps for QuickSort are as follows:
Divide: If |S| > 1, select a pivot element x in S and create
three sequences: L, E, and G where
LstoreselementsinS thatarelessthanx,
E storeselementsinS thatareequaltox,and
GstoreselementsinS thataregreaterthanx.
Recur: Recursively sort the sequences L and G.
Conquer: Put sorted elements from L, E, and G together
to form a sorted list.

QuickSort Tree
1. Split using pivotx
E = x
2. Recur 2. Recur
L < x R > x
19/31
3. Concatenate

--- Page 22 ---
Randomized QuickSort
The worst case running time of QuickSort occurs when one of
the sublists is in fact empty.
To avoid this worst case run time, we want to ensure that each
of L and G are sufficiently large for each recursive call.
For any deterministic method of choosing a pivot element, we
can design some list so that the running time is (almost) n2.
Solution: Select a pivot element randomly!
22/31
--- Page 23 ---
Randomized QuickSort (cont.)
Recall that our proposed method for the randomized QuickSort
algorithm is to choose the pivot element at random.
Wecanrepeattheselectionuntilwefinda“good”pivotelement
(defined below). With a good pivot element during each step,
wehaveanalgorithmwith(expected)runningtimethatiso(n2).
Theorem The expected running time of randomized QuickSort
(pivot chosen at random) is O(nlogn).
Proof idea:
We say that a random pivot is good if the size of neither L
or G is larger than 3|S|.
4
23/31
--- Page 24 ---
Randomized QuickSort (cont.)
The probability of success of choosing a good pivot is 1.
2
Fact: The expected number of times that a fair coin must
be tossed until it shows heads k times is 2k. Choosing a
good pivot is analogous to flipping a fair coin until it comes
up heads. So, on average we need two attempts (random
choices) to find a good pivot.
Any path in the QuickSort algorithm can contain at most
log n levels with good pivots.
4/3
Hence, the expected length of each path will be 2log n.
4/3
24/31
--- Page 25 ---
Randomized QuickSort
25/31
--- Page 26 ---
Selection
Selection problem: Given an (unsorted) list/array A of n
numbersandanintegeri ∈ {1,...,n},findthei’thsmallest
element.
Special cases:
i =1: minimum,i =n: maximum,i =⌈n/2⌉: median.
Naïve method: sort A and return the ith element. Running
time: O(nlogn).
Question: can we do better if we only want one order
statistic?
Answer: yes, Randomized-Select runs in expected O(n)
time.
26/31
--- Page 27 ---
A special case
Suppose i = n−1, that is, you want the second largest
element.
You can find the maximum in worst-case n−1
comparisons as we saw in Week 1.
Then among all the other n−1 elements you can find the
maximum (which is the overall second largest) in
worst-case n−2 comparisons.
Total of 2n−3 comparisons, and each comparison takes
O(1) time, so we get an O(n) algorithm, which can’t be
improved upon.
But if you care about the leading constant, there’s actually
a much faster algorithm.
27/31
--- Page 28 ---
Randomized Select
Randomized-Select is closely related to Randomized
QuickSort.
Pick a uniformly random pivot x ∈ A and partition:
L = {a ∈ A : a < x},E = {a ∈ A : a = x},G = {a ∈ A : a > x}.
Let k = |L|+1 be the rank of the pivot (assuming distinct
keys for simplicity).
If i = k, return x.
If i < k, recurse on L; if i > k, recurse on G with index
i −k.
Key difference with QuickSort: we recurse on only one
side, whereas in QuickSort we recursed on both sides.
28/31
--- Page 29 ---
Randomized Select, pseudocode
RANDOMIZED-SELECT(A,i)
if |A| = 1 then return the only element of A
choose pivot x uniformly at random from A
partition A into L,E,G where L < x, E = x, G > x
k ← |L|+1 // rank of pivot
if i = k then return x
else if i < k then return RANDOMIZED-SELECT(L,i)
else return RANDOMIZED-SELECT(G,i −k)
29/31
--- Page 30 ---
Running time analysis
Worst case: the pivot is always the smallest/largest
element.
Subproblemsizes: n−1,n−2,...,1
Totaltime: O(n2)
But the pivot is chosen uniformly at random.
Intuition: with constant probability the pivot is “good” (not
too closeto the ends), so the recursivesubproblem shrinks
by a constant factor.
Theorem: The expected running time of Randomized Select is
O(n).
Proof idea:
Just as in the analysis of QuickSort, we say that a random
pivot isgood if thesize of neither L orG islarger than 3|S|.
4
30/31
--- Page 31 ---
Deterministic Select
There is also a deterministic selection algorithm with
worst-case O(n) running time.
Main idea:
1 SplitAintogroupsof5elements.
2 Computethemedianofeachgroup.
3 Recursivelyfindthemedianofthesemedians;callitm.
4 UsemaspivotandpartitionAintoL,E,G.
One can prove m is always a “good pivot”.
In practice: Randomized Select is simpler and usually
faster; deterministic select is mainly of theoretical interest.
31/31
